Yeah, um… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially around my jaw. It’s like this sagging, uneven look on one side of my face that I can’t stop noticing.

Everyone else seemed to think the pictures were fine, but I kept zooming in and looking at my jawline and the side of my face, thinking, “Is that really how I look?” My wife tells me she barely notices anything, and even clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I still feel like it’s obvious. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It does interfere, yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and I’m not, like, staying in bed all day or anything. I’m a financial advisor, so I have to meet people and be put together, and I do get through the meetings. But it’s been slowing me down more than I’d like to admit.

Like before client appointments, especially if I know it’s in person, I’ll spend too long checking how my face looks from different angles, trying to see if the jaw thing is noticeable in the mirror or on my phone camera. And then I end up leaving later than I planned, or I’m kind of rushed and already tense by the time I get there.

During some appointments, I can still do my job, but I’m not as present as I should be. Part of my mind is on whether they’re looking at that side of my face, or whether the lighting is making it worse. I’ll catch myself thinking about my jaw when I should be fully listening.

And socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife and I sometimes go to those, and there are always people taking pictures, posting things, that kind of thing. I just didn’t want to be seen or photographed. I told myself I was tired or busy, but really it was because I didn’t want to deal with how I might look. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely taking up space in my life.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. More than I really want to admit, honestly.

Before client meetings is when it’s probably the most obvious. I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, then I’ll use my phone camera because somehow the mirror doesn’t feel like enough. And then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle, like turning my head a little to see if that side of my jaw looks more uneven. Sometimes I’ll mess with the lighting too, like step closer to the overhead light or away from it, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s just bad lighting or if that’s really how it looks.

And it’s not just checking once. It can be several times before I leave. I’ll adjust my collar, or try to position my face a certain way, like maybe if my shirt sits differently it draws less attention to it. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels important.

And yeah, I compare too. If I’m around other advisors, especially younger guys, I notice their jawlines or how their faces look more… firm, I guess. At gallery events too, I’ll catch myself looking at other people’s faces instead of just having a normal conversation. I’ll be standing there nodding along, but part of me is thinking, “His face looks normal, mine probably looks off.” It’s distracting, and then I feel embarrassed that I’m even doing it.

It’s like this sagging, uneven look on one side of my face that I can’t stop noticing.\n\nIt got worse after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. Everyone else seemed to think the pictures were fine, but I kept zooming in and looking at my jawline and the side of my face, thinking, “Is that really how I look?” My wife tells me she barely notices anything, and even clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I still feel like it’s obvious. I know that probably sounds like a small thing, but it’s been hard for me to let it go once I see it.\n\nDoctor: How much does this concern about your jawline interfere with your daily life or cause you distress?\n\nPatient: It does interfere, yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and I’m not, like, staying in bed all day or anything. I’m a financial advisor, so I have to meet people and be put together, and I do get through the meetings. But it’s been slowing me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before client appointments, especially if I know it’s in person, I’ll spend too long checking how my face looks from different angles, trying to see if the jaw thing is noticeable in the mirror or on my phone camera. And then I end up leaving later than I planned, or I’m kind of rushed and already tense by the time I get there.\n\nDuring some appointments, I can still do my job, but I’m not as present as I should be. Part of my mind is on whether they’re looking at that side of my face, or whether the lighting is making it worse. I’ll catch myself thinking about my jaw when I should be fully listening.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife and I sometimes go to those, and there are always people taking pictures, posting things, that kind of thing. I just didn’t want to be seen or photographed. I told myself I was tired or busy, but really it was because I didn’t want to deal with how I might look. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely taking up space in my life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your jaw looks, do you find yourself doing things like checking mirrors, taking pictures, or comparing your face to others repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I really want to admit, honestly.\n\nBefore client meetings is when it’s probably the most obvious. I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, then I’ll use my phone camera because somehow the mirror doesn’t feel like enough. And then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle, like turning my head a little to see if that side of my jaw looks more uneven. Sometimes I’ll mess with the lighting too, like step closer to the overhead light or away from it, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s just bad lighting or if that’s really how it looks.\n\nAnd it’s not just checking once. It can be several times before I leave. I’ll adjust my collar, or try to position my face a certain way, like maybe if my shirt sits differently it draws less attention to it. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels important.\n\nAnd yeah, I compare too. If I’m around other advisors, especially younger guys, I notice their jawlines or how their faces look more… firm, I guess. At gallery events too, I’ll catch myself looking at other people’s faces instead of just having a normal conversation. I’ll be standing there nodding along, but part of me is thinking, “His face looks normal, mine probably looks off.” It’s distracting, and then I feel embarrassed that I’m even doing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over again when you’re concerned about your jawline, like checking multiple times, trying to fix it mentally, or going over it again and again in your head?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / personal_financial_advisor / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before client meetings I’ll check the bathroom mirror and my phone camera several times, adjust the lighting, maybe change my collar or angle my face, and then I catch myself comparing my face to younger advisors or people at gallery events instead of focusing on the conversation.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. It becomes this whole loop, especially before I’m about to see clients in person. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just check once and go,” but then I end up in the bathroom looking in the mirror, turning my head slightly one way and then the other, trying to see if the jaw looks uneven or saggy from that side.

And then the mirror doesn’t feel reliable enough, so I’ll pull out my phone camera and check that too. Sometimes I’ll move closer to the light or step back, or tilt my face, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s the lighting making it look worse or if that’s just how I actually look. Then I’ll adjust my collar, maybe try to hold my face at a certain angle, like somehow that’s going to make it less noticeable.

And even after I leave, it keeps going in my head. If I’m with younger advisors or at a gallery event, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of paying attention to what’s being said. I’ll be nodding along, but mentally I’m thinking, “His jaw looks normal,” or “I wonder if mine looks worse from this angle.” It’s embarrassing, because I know I should just be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps going back to it.

It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely taking up space in my life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your jaw looks, do you find yourself doing things like checking mirrors, taking pictures, or comparing your face to others repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I really want to admit, honestly.\n\nBefore client meetings is when it’s probably the most obvious. I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, then I’ll use my phone camera because somehow the mirror doesn’t feel like enough. And then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle, like turning my head a little to see if that side of my jaw looks more uneven. Sometimes I’ll mess with the lighting too, like step closer to the overhead light or away from it, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s just bad lighting or if that’s really how it looks.\n\nAnd it’s not just checking once. It can be several times before I leave. I’ll adjust my collar, or try to position my face a certain way, like maybe if my shirt sits differently it draws less attention to it. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels important.\n\nAnd yeah, I compare too. If I’m around other advisors, especially younger guys, I notice their jawlines or how their faces look more… firm, I guess. At gallery events too, I’ll catch myself looking at other people’s faces instead of just having a normal conversation. I’ll be standing there nodding along, but part of me is thinking, “His face looks normal, mine probably looks off.” It’s distracting, and then I feel embarrassed that I’m even doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over again when you’re concerned about your jawline, like checking multiple times, trying to fix it mentally, or going over it again and again in your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It becomes this whole loop, especially before I’m about to see clients in person. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just check once and go,” but then I end up in the bathroom looking in the mirror, turning my head slightly one way and then the other, trying to see if the jaw looks uneven or saggy from that side.\n\nAnd then the mirror doesn’t feel reliable enough, so I’ll pull out my phone camera and check that too. Sometimes I’ll move closer to the light or step back, or tilt my face, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s the lighting making it look worse or if that’s just how I actually look. Then I’ll adjust my collar, maybe try to hold my face at a certain angle, like somehow that’s going to make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd even after I leave, it keeps going in my head. If I’m with younger advisors or at a gallery event, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of paying attention to what’s being said. I’ll be nodding along, but mentally I’m thinking, “His jaw looks normal,” or “I wonder if mine looks worse from this angle.” It’s embarrassing, because I know I should just be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps going back to it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever avoided certain situations, like social events or even work-related activities, because you were so concerned about how your jawline looks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / personal_financial_advisor / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I still get through my work, but I’ve been slower getting out the door for client meetings, less present during some appointments, and I’ve skipped a few art openings because I didn’t want to be seen or photographed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I have. Not constantly, but enough that it bothers me.

Work-wise, I don’t usually outright cancel client meetings because of it—I still go, I still do my job—but it affects the whole lead-up. I’ll get stuck checking my face before I leave, and then suddenly I’m running behind or I’m leaving the house tense and irritated with myself. So I’m technically showing up, but not in the calm, prepared way I’d like to. And during some appointments I can tell I’m not fully there, because part of me is still worrying about whether the client is noticing my jaw or how I look from their side of the table.

Socially, I’ve avoided more. My wife and I sometimes go to art openings, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to be seen, or photographed, or end up in someone’s post looking bad. I usually give some normal excuse, like I’m tired or I have work to catch up on, but if I’m being honest, it’s really because I don’t want to deal with feeling exposed like that.

So it’s not like I’ve stopped living my life, but yes, I do avoid some things, and even when I don’t avoid them, it slows me down and takes me out of the moment.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your jaw looks, do you find yourself doing things like checking mirrors, taking pictures, or comparing your face to others repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I really want to admit, honestly.\n\nBefore client meetings is when it’s probably the most obvious. I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, then I’ll use my phone camera because somehow the mirror doesn’t feel like enough. And then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle, like turning my head a little to see if that side of my jaw looks more uneven. Sometimes I’ll mess with the lighting too, like step closer to the overhead light or away from it, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s just bad lighting or if that’s really how it looks.\n\nAnd it’s not just checking once. It can be several times before I leave. I’ll adjust my collar, or try to position my face a certain way, like maybe if my shirt sits differently it draws less attention to it. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels important.\n\nAnd yeah, I compare too. If I’m around other advisors, especially younger guys, I notice their jawlines or how their faces look more… firm, I guess. At gallery events too, I’ll catch myself looking at other people’s faces instead of just having a normal conversation. I’ll be standing there nodding along, but part of me is thinking, “His face looks normal, mine probably looks off.” It’s distracting, and then I feel embarrassed that I’m even doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over again when you’re concerned about your jawline, like checking multiple times, trying to fix it mentally, or going over it again and again in your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It becomes this whole loop, especially before I’m about to see clients in person. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just check once and go,” but then I end up in the bathroom looking in the mirror, turning my head slightly one way and then the other, trying to see if the jaw looks uneven or saggy from that side.\n\nAnd then the mirror doesn’t feel reliable enough, so I’ll pull out my phone camera and check that too. Sometimes I’ll move closer to the light or step back, or tilt my face, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s the lighting making it look worse or if that’s just how I actually look. Then I’ll adjust my collar, maybe try to hold my face at a certain angle, like somehow that’s going to make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd even after I leave, it keeps going in my head. If I’m with younger advisors or at a gallery event, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of paying attention to what’s being said. I’ll be nodding along, but mentally I’m thinking, “His jaw looks normal,” or “I wonder if mine looks worse from this angle.” It’s embarrassing, because I know I should just be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps going back to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided certain situations, like social events or even work-related activities, because you were so concerned about how your jawline looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Not constantly, but enough that it bothers me.\n\nWork-wise, I don’t usually outright cancel client meetings because of it—I still go, I still do my job—but it affects the whole lead-up. I’ll get stuck checking my face before I leave, and then suddenly I’m running behind or I’m leaving the house tense and irritated with myself. So I’m technically showing up, but not in the calm, prepared way I’d like to. And during some appointments I can tell I’m not fully there, because part of me is still worrying about whether the client is noticing my jaw or how I look from their side of the table.\n\nSocially, I’ve avoided more. My wife and I sometimes go to art openings, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to be seen, or photographed, or end up in someone’s post looking bad. I usually give some normal excuse, like I’m tired or I have work to catch up on, but if I’m being honest, it’s really because I don’t want to deal with feeling exposed like that.\n\nSo it’s not like I’ve stopped living my life, but yes, I do avoid some things, and even when I don’t avoid them, it slows me down and takes me out of the moment.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're concerned about how your jawline looks, do you find yourself doing things over and over again — like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or trying to mentally 'fix' how you think you look — in order to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 45 / personal_financial_advisor / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors\nmanifestation: \"Before client meetings I’ll check the bathroom mirror and my phone camera several times, adjust the lighting, maybe change my collar or angle my face, and then I catch myself comparing my face to younger advisors or people at gallery events instead of focusing on the conversation.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. It’s not just a quick glance and then I move on. It turns into this whole routine, especially before client meetings.

I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, and then I’ll think, “Well, maybe that angle isn’t accurate,” so I’ll pull out my phone camera and look there too. Then I’ll turn my head a little, check one side, then the other side, and I’ll mess with the lighting—like move closer to the overhead light or step back—because I’m trying to decide if my jaw really looks off or if it’s just shadows making it look worse.

And then I’ll start adjusting things, like my collar or the way my shirt sits, almost like if I can frame my face differently it won’t stand out as much. Sometimes I’ll kind of hold my face or posture a certain way, which is hard to explain without sounding strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m trying to make it look more normal before I go in front of people.

The mental part keeps going too. If I’m around younger advisors, or at gallery events, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of actually listening. I’ll be looking at someone’s jawline or profile and thinking, “Okay, his looks normal, mine probably doesn’t,” and then I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out how bad mine looks. It’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps circling back to it.

Then I’ll adjust my collar, maybe try to hold my face at a certain angle, like somehow that’s going to make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd even after I leave, it keeps going in my head. I’ll be nodding along, but mentally I’m thinking, “His jaw looks normal,” or “I wonder if mine looks worse from this angle.” It’s embarrassing, because I know I should just be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps going back to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided certain situations, like social events or even work-related activities, because you were so concerned about how your jawline looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Not constantly, but enough that it bothers me.\n\nWork-wise, I don’t usually outright cancel client meetings because of it—I still go, I still do my job—but it affects the whole lead-up. I’ll get stuck checking my face before I leave, and then suddenly I’m running behind or I’m leaving the house tense and irritated with myself. So I’m technically showing up, but not in the calm, prepared way I’d like to. And during some appointments I can tell I’m not fully there, because part of me is still worrying about whether the client is noticing my jaw or how I look from their side of the table.\n\nSocially, I’ve avoided more. My wife and I sometimes go to art openings, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to be seen, or photographed, or end up in someone’s post looking bad. I usually give some normal excuse, like I’m tired or I have work to catch up on, but if I’m being honest, it’s really because I don’t want to deal with feeling exposed like that.\n\nSo it’s not like I’ve stopped living my life, but yes, I do avoid some things, and even when I don’t avoid them, it slows me down and takes me out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're concerned about how your jawline looks, do you find yourself doing things over and over again — like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or trying to mentally 'fix' how you think you look — in order to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not just a quick glance and then I move on. It turns into this whole routine, especially before client meetings.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, and then I’ll think, “Well, maybe that angle isn’t accurate,” so I’ll pull out my phone camera and look there too. Then I’ll turn my head a little, check one side, then the other side, and I’ll mess with the lighting—like move closer to the overhead light or step back—because I’m trying to decide if my jaw really looks off or if it’s just shadows making it look worse.\n\nAnd then I’ll start adjusting things, like my collar or the way my shirt sits, almost like if I can frame my face differently it won’t stand out as much. Sometimes I’ll kind of hold my face or posture a certain way, which is hard to explain without sounding strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m trying to make it look more normal before I go in front of people.\n\nThe mental part keeps going too. If I’m around younger advisors, or at gallery events, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of actually listening. I’ll be looking at someone’s jawline or profile and thinking, “Okay, his looks normal, mine probably doesn’t,” and then I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out how bad mine looks. It’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel anxious or fearful in situations where people might be paying attention to you, even if it's not about how your face looks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 7\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure. I mean, I can get a little tense if I’m “on,” like if I’m speaking with clients or I know people are looking to me to explain something clearly, but I’ve always thought of that as pretty normal work pressure.

It’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. If people are paying attention to me, my mind usually goes pretty quickly to how I’m coming across physically, so I don’t know if I’m anxious about attention in general or if it just gets tied up with that. Like, if someone is listening closely, I’m not usually thinking, “They’re judging my personality” or “I’m going to embarrass myself.” It’s more… I become aware that I’m being seen.

So, outside of the appearance piece, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a big fear of attention by itself. I can do meetings, I can talk to people, I can handle being in a professional role. It’s just when that attention makes me feel visible that I start getting uncomfortable.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially around my jaw. It’s like this sagging, uneven look on one side of my face that I can’t stop noticing.\n\nIt got worse after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. Everyone else seemed to think the pictures were fine, but I kept zooming in and looking at my jawline and the side of my face, thinking, “Is that really how I look?” My wife tells me she barely notices anything, and even clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I still feel like it’s obvious. I know that probably sounds like a small thing, but it’s been hard for me to let it go once I see it.\n\nDoctor: How much does this concern about your jawline interfere with your daily life or cause you distress?\n\nPatient: It does interfere, yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and I’m not, like, staying in bed all day or anything. I’m a financial advisor, so I have to meet people and be put together, and I do get through the meetings. But it’s been slowing me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before client appointments, especially if I know it’s in person, I’ll spend too long checking how my face looks from different angles, trying to see if the jaw thing is noticeable in the mirror or on my phone camera. And then I end up leaving later than I planned, or I’m kind of rushed and already tense by the time I get there.\n\nDuring some appointments, I can still do my job, but I’m not as present as I should be. Part of my mind is on whether they’re looking at that side of my face, or whether the lighting is making it worse. I’ll catch myself thinking about my jaw when I should be fully listening.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife and I sometimes go to those, and there are always people taking pictures, posting things, that kind of thing. I just didn’t want to be seen or photographed. I told myself I was tired or busy, but really it was because I didn’t want to deal with how I might look. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely taking up space in my life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your jaw looks, do you find yourself doing things like checking mirrors, taking pictures, or comparing your face to others repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I really want to admit, honestly.\n\nBefore client meetings is when it’s probably the most obvious. I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, then I’ll use my phone camera because somehow the mirror doesn’t feel like enough. And then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle, like turning my head a little to see if that side of my jaw looks more uneven. Sometimes I’ll mess with the lighting too, like step closer to the overhead light or away from it, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s just bad lighting or if that’s really how it looks.\n\nAnd it’s not just checking once. It can be several times before I leave. I’ll adjust my collar, or try to position my face a certain way, like maybe if my shirt sits differently it draws less attention to it. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels important.\n\nAnd yeah, I compare too. If I’m around other advisors, especially younger guys, I notice their jawlines or how their faces look more… firm, I guess. At gallery events too, I’ll catch myself looking at other people’s faces instead of just having a normal conversation. I’ll be standing there nodding along, but part of me is thinking, “His face looks normal, mine probably looks off.” It’s distracting, and then I feel embarrassed that I’m even doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over again when you’re concerned about your jawline, like checking multiple times, trying to fix it mentally, or going over it again and again in your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It becomes this whole loop, especially before I’m about to see clients in person. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just check once and go,” but then I end up in the bathroom looking in the mirror, turning my head slightly one way and then the other, trying to see if the jaw looks uneven or saggy from that side.\n\nAnd then the mirror doesn’t feel reliable enough, so I’ll pull out my phone camera and check that too. I usually give some normal excuse, like I’m tired or I have work to catch up on, but if I’m being honest, it’s really because I don’t want to deal with feeling exposed like that.\n\nSo it’s not like I’ve stopped living my life, but yes, I do avoid some things, and even when I don’t avoid them, it slows me down and takes me out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're concerned about how your jawline looks, do you find yourself doing things over and over again — like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or trying to mentally 'fix' how you think you look — in order to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not just a quick glance and then I move on. It turns into this whole routine, especially before client meetings.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, and then I’ll think, “Well, maybe that angle isn’t accurate,” so I’ll pull out my phone camera and look there too. Then I’ll turn my head a little, check one side, then the other side, and I’ll mess with the lighting—like move closer to the overhead light or step back—because I’m trying to decide if my jaw really looks off or if it’s just shadows making it look worse.\n\nAnd then I’ll start adjusting things, like my collar or the way my shirt sits, almost like if I can frame my face differently it won’t stand out as much. Sometimes I’ll kind of hold my face or posture a certain way, which is hard to explain without sounding strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m trying to make it look more normal before I go in front of people.\n\nThe mental part keeps going too. If I’m around younger advisors, or at gallery events, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of actually listening. I’ll be looking at someone’s jawline or profile and thinking, “Okay, his looks normal, mine probably doesn’t,” and then I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out how bad mine looks. It’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful in situations where people might be paying attention to you, even if it's not about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I can get a little tense if I’m “on,” like if I’m speaking with clients or I know people are looking to me to explain something clearly, but I’ve always thought of that as pretty normal work pressure.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. If people are paying attention to me, my mind usually goes pretty quickly to how I’m coming across physically, so I don’t know if I’m anxious about attention in general or if it just gets tied up with that. Like, if someone is listening closely, I’m not usually thinking, “They’re judging my personality” or “I’m going to embarrass myself.” It’s more… I become aware that I’m being seen.\n\nSo, outside of the appearance piece, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a big fear of attention by itself. I can do meetings, I can talk to people, I can handle being in a professional role. It’s just when that attention makes me feel visible that I start getting uncomfortable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, meeting new people can be a little awkward at first, but I don’t usually feel afraid of it on its own. If I’m introduced to someone at a work thing or a dinner or one of my wife’s art events, I can make conversation. I may feel pressure to sound competent, but that feels different from social anxiety, I guess. It’s more like I want to do my job well.

So outside of the appearance part, I haven’t really noticed a separate pattern where I avoid people or panic about social situations. It’s more that if attention is on me, I become aware of being looked at, and then it can turn into that other thing. But just meeting people or talking to people, no, not really in itself.

So I’m technically showing up, but not in the calm, prepared way I’d like to. Yes, it does. I mean, even if no one says anything, that’s almost what makes it worse sometimes, because then I’m trying to read their faces and figure out whether they noticed.

What really brought me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It started feeling much more real to me after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. I know photos can catch you at bad angles, and my wife tells me she barely notices anything, and clients obviously aren’t saying anything about it… but in my head it feels obvious. Like if I’m sitting across from someone, I’ll think, “They can see that side of my face looks lower,” or “My jawline looks soft and uneven,” and then I get really tense.

It’s not that people are commenting directly. They usually aren’t. That’s part of why I feel a little ridiculous explaining it. But I still feel distressed because I’m convinced they might be noticing it silently. My wife will say, “You look fine,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” and I want to believe her, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It just keeps coming back, especially if I’m in a situation where I feel visible.

But it’s been slowing me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before client appointments, especially if I know it’s in person, I’ll spend too long checking how my face looks from different angles, trying to see if the jaw thing is noticeable in the mirror or on my phone camera. And then I end up leaving later than I planned, or I’m kind of rushed and already tense by the time I get there.\n\nDuring some appointments, I can still do my job, but I’m not as present as I should be. Part of my mind is on whether they’re looking at that side of my face, or whether the lighting is making it worse. I’ll catch myself thinking about my jaw when I should be fully listening.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife and I sometimes go to those, and there are always people taking pictures, posting things, that kind of thing. I just didn’t want to be seen or photographed. I told myself I was tired or busy, but really it was because I didn’t want to deal with how I might look. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely taking up space in my life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your jaw looks, do you find yourself doing things like checking mirrors, taking pictures, or comparing your face to others repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I really want to admit, honestly.\n\nBefore client meetings is when it’s probably the most obvious. I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, then I’ll use my phone camera because somehow the mirror doesn’t feel like enough. And then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle, like turning my head a little to see if that side of my jaw looks more uneven. Sometimes I’ll mess with the lighting too, like step closer to the overhead light or away from it, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s just bad lighting or if that’s really how it looks.\n\nAnd it’s not just checking once. It can be several times before I leave. I’ll adjust my collar, or try to position my face a certain way, like maybe if my shirt sits differently it draws less attention to it. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels important.\n\nAnd yeah, I compare too. If I’m around other advisors, especially younger guys, I notice their jawlines or how their faces look more… firm, I guess. At gallery events too, I’ll catch myself looking at other people’s faces instead of just having a normal conversation. I’ll be standing there nodding along, but part of me is thinking, “His face looks normal, mine probably looks off.” It’s distracting, and then I feel embarrassed that I’m even doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over again when you’re concerned about your jawline, like checking multiple times, trying to fix it mentally, or going over it again and again in your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It becomes this whole loop, especially before I’m about to see clients in person. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just check once and go,” but then I end up in the bathroom looking in the mirror, turning my head slightly one way and then the other, trying to see if the jaw looks uneven or saggy from that side.\n\nAnd then the mirror doesn’t feel reliable enough, so I’ll pull out my phone camera and check that too. Sometimes I’ll move closer to the light or step back, or tilt my face, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s the lighting making it look worse or if that’s just how I actually look. Then I’ll adjust my collar, maybe try to hold my face at a certain angle, like somehow that’s going to make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd even after I leave, it keeps going in my head. If I’m with younger advisors or at a gallery event, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of paying attention to what’s being said. I’ll be nodding along, but mentally I’m thinking, “His jaw looks normal,” or “I wonder if mine looks worse from this angle.” It’s embarrassing, because I know I should just be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps going back to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided certain situations, like social events or even work-related activities, because you were so concerned about how your jawline looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Not constantly, but enough that it bothers me.\n\nWork-wise, I don’t usually outright cancel client meetings because of it—I still go, I still do my job—but it affects the whole lead-up. I’ll get stuck checking my face before I leave, and then suddenly I’m running behind or I’m leaving the house tense and irritated with myself. So I’m technically showing up, but not in the calm, prepared way I’d like to. And during some appointments I can tell I’m not fully there, because part of me is still worrying about whether the client is noticing my jaw or how I look from their side of the table.\n\nSocially, I’ve avoided more. My wife and I sometimes go to art openings, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to be seen, or photographed, or end up in someone’s post looking bad. I usually give some normal excuse, like I’m tired or I have work to catch up on, but if I’m being honest, it’s really because I don’t want to deal with feeling exposed like that.\n\nSo it’s not like I’ve stopped living my life, but yes, I do avoid some things, and even when I don’t avoid them, it slows me down and takes me out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're concerned about how your jawline looks, do you find yourself doing things over and over again — like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or trying to mentally 'fix' how you think you look — in order to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not just a quick glance and then I move on. It turns into this whole routine, especially before client meetings.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, and then I’ll think, “Well, maybe that angle isn’t accurate,” so I’ll pull out my phone camera and look there too. Then I’ll turn my head a little, check one side, then the other side, and I’ll mess with the lighting—like move closer to the overhead light or step back—because I’m trying to decide if my jaw really looks off or if it’s just shadows making it look worse.\n\nAnd then I’ll start adjusting things, like my collar or the way my shirt sits, almost like if I can frame my face differently it won’t stand out as much. Sometimes I’ll kind of hold my face or posture a certain way, which is hard to explain without sounding strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m trying to make it look more normal before I go in front of people.\n\nThe mental part keeps going too. If I’m around younger advisors, or at gallery events, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of actually listening. I’ll be looking at someone’s jawline or profile and thinking, “Okay, his looks normal, mine probably doesn’t,” and then I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out how bad mine looks. It’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful in situations where people might be paying attention to you, even if it's not about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I can get a little tense if I’m “on,” like if I’m speaking with clients or I know people are looking to me to explain something clearly, but I’ve always thought of that as pretty normal work pressure.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. If people are paying attention to me, my mind usually goes pretty quickly to how I’m coming across physically, so I don’t know if I’m anxious about attention in general or if it just gets tied up with that. Like, if someone is listening closely, I’m not usually thinking, “They’re judging my personality” or “I’m going to embarrass myself.” It’s more… I become aware that I’m being seen.\n\nSo, outside of the appearance piece, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a big fear of attention by itself. I can do meetings, I can talk to people, I can handle being in a professional role. It’s just when that attention makes me feel visible that I start getting uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Outside of concerns about your appearance, do you ever feel anxious or self-conscious in social situations, like meeting new people or being the center of attention?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, meeting new people can be a little awkward at first, but I don’t usually feel afraid of it on its own. If I’m introduced to someone at a work thing or a dinner or one of my wife’s art events, I can make conversation. I might be a bit stiff at the beginning, but I don’t think I’m sitting there worrying that I’ll say something humiliating or that they’ll dislike me as a person.\n\nBeing the center of attention is… I don’t love it, exactly, but I can handle it. Like if I have to explain something to clients or speak in a small group, I can do that. I may feel pressure to sound competent, but that feels different from social anxiety, I guess. It’s more like I want to do my job well.\n\nSo outside of the appearance part, I haven’t really noticed a separate pattern where I avoid people or panic about social situations. It’s more that if attention is on me, I become aware of being looked at, and then it can turn into that other thing. But just meeting people or talking to people, no, not really in itself.\n\nDoctor: When you're in situations where you feel others might be noticing how you look, does it cause you a lot of anxiety or distress, even if they’re not directly commenting on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does. I mean, even if no one says anything, that’s almost what makes it worse sometimes, because then I’m trying to read their faces and figure out whether they noticed.\n\nWhat really brought me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It started feeling much more real to me after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. I know photos can catch you at bad angles, and my wife tells me she barely notices anything, and clients obviously aren’t saying anything about it… but in my head it feels obvious. Like if I’m sitting across from someone, I’ll think, “They can see that side of my face looks lower,” or “My jawline looks soft and uneven,” and then I get really tense.\n\nIt’s not that people are commenting directly. They usually aren’t. That’s part of why I feel a little ridiculous explaining it. But I still feel distressed because I’m convinced they might be noticing it silently. My wife will say, “You look fine,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” and I want to believe her, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It just keeps coming back, especially if I’m in a situation where I feel visible.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F45.22", "F41.1"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful in situations where people might be paying attention to you, even if it's not about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I can get a little tense if I’m “on,” like if I’m speaking with clients or I know people are looking to me to explain something clearly, but I’ve always thought of that as pretty normal work pressure.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. If people are paying attention to me, my mind usually goes pretty quickly to how I’m coming across physically, so I don’t know if I’m anxious about attention in general or if it just gets tied up with that. Like, if someone is listening closely, I’m not usually thinking, “They’re judging my personality” or “I’m going to embarrass myself.” It’s more… I become aware that I’m being seen.\n\nSo, outside of the appearance piece, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a big fear of attention by itself. I can do meetings, I can talk to people, I can handle being in a professional role. It’s just when that attention makes me feel visible that I start getting uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Outside of concerns about your appearance, do you ever feel anxious or self-conscious in social situations, like meeting new people or being the center of attention?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, meeting new people can be a little awkward at first, but I don’t usually feel afraid of it on its own. If I’m introduced to someone at a work thing or a dinner or one of my wife’s art events, I can make conversation. I might be a bit stiff at the beginning, but I don’t think I’m sitting there worrying that I’ll say something humiliating or that they’ll dislike me as a person.\n\nBeing the center of attention is… I don’t love it, exactly, but I can handle it. It’s more like I want to do my job well.\n\nSo outside of the appearance part, I haven’t really noticed a separate pattern where I avoid people or panic about social situations. It’s more that if attention is on me, I become aware of being looked at, and then it can turn into that other thing. It started feeling much more real to me after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. I know photos can catch you at bad angles, and my wife tells me she barely notices anything, and clients obviously aren’t saying anything about it… but in my head it feels obvious. They usually aren’t. That’s part of why I feel a little ridiculous explaining it. But I still feel distressed because I’m convinced they might be noticing it silently. My wife will say, “You look fine,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” and I want to believe her, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I go back and forth, but if I’m being honest, in the moment it feels like other people could notice it. Like the sagging or unevenness around my jawline and one side of my face feels so obvious to me once I’ve locked onto it that I have a hard time believing no one else can see it.

At the same time, I know that sounds… maybe not completely logical, because my wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t comment on it or seem to react in any obvious way. But that doesn’t really reassure me for long. I’ll think, well, maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they notice but wouldn’t say anything because that would be rude.

It really got worse after I saw myself in those family photos. I kept seeing this sagging, uneven look, especially around the jaw and one side of my face, and now it’s like I can’t unsee it. So part of me knows it might not be obvious to everyone else, or maybe not as bad as I think, but the stronger feeling is that people probably do notice it and are just not saying anything.

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because I’ve been getting really stuck on how my face looks, especially around my jaw. It’s like this sagging, uneven look on one side of my face that I can’t stop noticing.\n\nIt got worse after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. Everyone else seemed to think the pictures were fine, but I kept zooming in and looking at my jawline and the side of my face, thinking, “Is that really how I look?” My wife tells me she barely notices anything, and even clients don’t seem to react or anything, but I still feel like it’s obvious. I know that probably sounds like a small thing, but it’s been hard for me to let it go once I see it.\n\nDoctor: How much does this concern about your jawline interfere with your daily life or cause you distress?\n\nPatient: It does interfere, yeah… I mean, I’m still functioning, I still go to work and I’m not, like, staying in bed all day or anything. I’m a financial advisor, so I have to meet people and be put together, and I do get through the meetings. But it’s been slowing me down more than I’d like to admit.\n\nLike before client appointments, especially if I know it’s in person, I’ll spend too long checking how my face looks from different angles, trying to see if the jaw thing is noticeable in the mirror or on my phone camera. And then I end up leaving later than I planned, or I’m kind of rushed and already tense by the time I get there.\n\nDuring some appointments, I can still do my job, but I’m not as present as I should be. Part of my mind is on whether they’re looking at that side of my face, or whether the lighting is making it worse. I’ll catch myself thinking about my jaw when I should be fully listening.\n\nAnd socially, I’ve skipped a few art openings. My wife and I sometimes go to those, and there are always people taking pictures, posting things, that kind of thing. I just didn’t want to be seen or photographed. I told myself I was tired or busy, but really it was because I didn’t want to deal with how I might look. It’s not ruining everything, but it’s definitely taking up space in my life.\n\nDoctor: When you’re worried about how your jaw looks, do you find yourself doing things like checking mirrors, taking pictures, or comparing your face to others repeatedly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. More than I really want to admit, honestly.\n\nBefore client meetings is when it’s probably the most obvious. I’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, then I’ll use my phone camera because somehow the mirror doesn’t feel like enough. And then I’ll check again from a slightly different angle, like turning my head a little to see if that side of my jaw looks more uneven. Sometimes I’ll mess with the lighting too, like step closer to the overhead light or away from it, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s just bad lighting or if that’s really how it looks.\n\nAnd it’s not just checking once. It can be several times before I leave. I’ll adjust my collar, or try to position my face a certain way, like maybe if my shirt sits differently it draws less attention to it. Which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels important.\n\nAnd yeah, I compare too. If I’m around other advisors, especially younger guys, I notice their jawlines or how their faces look more… firm, I guess. At gallery events too, I’ll catch myself looking at other people’s faces instead of just having a normal conversation. I’ll be standing there nodding along, but part of me is thinking, “His face looks normal, mine probably looks off.” It’s distracting, and then I feel embarrassed that I’m even doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or thoughts over and over again when you’re concerned about your jawline, like checking multiple times, trying to fix it mentally, or going over it again and again in your head?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It becomes this whole loop, especially before I’m about to see clients in person. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just check once and go,” but then I end up in the bathroom looking in the mirror, turning my head slightly one way and then the other, trying to see if the jaw looks uneven or saggy from that side.\n\nAnd then the mirror doesn’t feel reliable enough, so I’ll pull out my phone camera and check that too. Sometimes I’ll move closer to the light or step back, or tilt my face, because I’m trying to figure out if it’s the lighting making it look worse or if that’s just how I actually look. Then I’ll adjust my collar, maybe try to hold my face at a certain angle, like somehow that’s going to make it less noticeable.\n\nAnd even after I leave, it keeps going in my head. If I’m with younger advisors or at a gallery event, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of paying attention to what’s being said. I’ll be nodding along, but mentally I’m thinking, “His jaw looks normal,” or “I wonder if mine looks worse from this angle.” It’s embarrassing, because I know I should just be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps going back to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever avoided certain situations, like social events or even work-related activities, because you were so concerned about how your jawline looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Not constantly, but enough that it bothers me.\n\nWork-wise, I don’t usually outright cancel client meetings because of it—I still go, I still do my job—but it affects the whole lead-up. I’ll get stuck checking my face before I leave, and then suddenly I’m running behind or I’m leaving the house tense and irritated with myself. So I’m technically showing up, but not in the calm, prepared way I’d like to. And during some appointments I can tell I’m not fully there, because part of me is still worrying about whether the client is noticing my jaw or how I look from their side of the table.\n\nSocially, I’ve avoided more. My wife and I sometimes go to art openings, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to be seen, or photographed, or end up in someone’s post looking bad. I usually give some normal excuse, like I’m tired or I have work to catch up on, but if I’m being honest, it’s really because I don’t want to deal with feeling exposed like that.\n\nSo it’s not like I’ve stopped living my life, but yes, I do avoid some things, and even when I don’t avoid them, it slows me down and takes me out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're concerned about how your jawline looks, do you find yourself doing things over and over again — like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or trying to mentally 'fix' how you think you look — in order to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not just a quick glance and then I move on. It turns into this whole routine, especially before client meetings.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, and then I’ll think, “Well, maybe that angle isn’t accurate,” so I’ll pull out my phone camera and look there too. Then I’ll turn my head a little, check one side, then the other side, and I’ll mess with the lighting—like move closer to the overhead light or step back—because I’m trying to decide if my jaw really looks off or if it’s just shadows making it look worse.\n\nAnd then I’ll start adjusting things, like my collar or the way my shirt sits, almost like if I can frame my face differently it won’t stand out as much. Sometimes I’ll kind of hold my face or posture a certain way, which is hard to explain without sounding strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m trying to make it look more normal before I go in front of people.\n\nThe mental part keeps going too. If I’m around younger advisors, or at gallery events, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of actually listening. I’ll be looking at someone’s jawline or profile and thinking, “Okay, his looks normal, mine probably doesn’t,” and then I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out how bad mine looks. I usually give some normal excuse, like I’m tired or I have work to catch up on, but if I’m being honest, it’s really because I don’t want to deal with feeling exposed like that.\n\nSo it’s not like I’ve stopped living my life, but yes, I do avoid some things, and even when I don’t avoid them, it slows me down and takes me out of the moment.\n\nDoctor: When you're concerned about how your jawline looks, do you find yourself doing things over and over again — like checking your appearance, adjusting your posture, or trying to mentally 'fix' how you think you look — in order to feel reassured or to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s not just a quick glance and then I move on. It turns into this whole routine, especially before client meetings.\n\nI’ll go into the bathroom and check the mirror, and then I’ll think, “Well, maybe that angle isn’t accurate,” so I’ll pull out my phone camera and look there too. Then I’ll turn my head a little, check one side, then the other side, and I’ll mess with the lighting—like move closer to the overhead light or step back—because I’m trying to decide if my jaw really looks off or if it’s just shadows making it look worse.\n\nAnd then I’ll start adjusting things, like my collar or the way my shirt sits, almost like if I can frame my face differently it won’t stand out as much. Sometimes I’ll kind of hold my face or posture a certain way, which is hard to explain without sounding strange, but in the moment it feels like I’m trying to make it look more normal before I go in front of people.\n\nThe mental part keeps going too. If I’m around younger advisors, or at gallery events, I’ll catch myself comparing my face to theirs instead of actually listening. I’ll be looking at someone’s jawline or profile and thinking, “Okay, his looks normal, mine probably doesn’t,” and then I’m trying to reassure myself or figure out how bad mine looks. It’s embarrassing, because I know I’m supposed to be focused on the conversation, but my mind keeps circling back to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or fearful in situations where people might be paying attention to you, even if it's not about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I can get a little tense if I’m “on,” like if I’m speaking with clients or I know people are looking to me to explain something clearly, but I’ve always thought of that as pretty normal work pressure.\n\nIt’s hard for me to separate it out, honestly. If people are paying attention to me, my mind usually goes pretty quickly to how I’m coming across physically, so I don’t know if I’m anxious about attention in general or if it just gets tied up with that. Like, if someone is listening closely, I’m not usually thinking, “They’re judging my personality” or “I’m going to embarrass myself.” It’s more… I become aware that I’m being seen.\n\nSo, outside of the appearance piece, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a big fear of attention by itself. I can do meetings, I can talk to people, I can handle being in a professional role. It’s just when that attention makes me feel visible that I start getting uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Outside of concerns about your appearance, do you ever feel anxious or self-conscious in social situations, like meeting new people or being the center of attention?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I mean, meeting new people can be a little awkward at first, but I don’t usually feel afraid of it on its own. If I’m introduced to someone at a work thing or a dinner or one of my wife’s art events, I can make conversation. I might be a bit stiff at the beginning, but I don’t think I’m sitting there worrying that I’ll say something humiliating or that they’ll dislike me as a person.\n\nBeing the center of attention is… I don’t love it, exactly, but I can handle it. Like if I have to explain something to clients or speak in a small group, I can do that. I may feel pressure to sound competent, but that feels different from social anxiety, I guess. It’s more like I want to do my job well.\n\nSo outside of the appearance part, I haven’t really noticed a separate pattern where I avoid people or panic about social situations. It’s more that if attention is on me, I become aware of being looked at, and then it can turn into that other thing. But just meeting people or talking to people, no, not really in itself.\n\nDoctor: When you're in situations where you feel others might be noticing how you look, does it cause you a lot of anxiety or distress, even if they’re not directly commenting on your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does. I mean, even if no one says anything, that’s almost what makes it worse sometimes, because then I’m trying to read their faces and figure out whether they noticed.\n\nWhat really brought me in is that I keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face. It started feeling much more real to me after I saw myself in a bunch of family photos. I know photos can catch you at bad angles, and my wife tells me she barely notices anything, and clients obviously aren’t saying anything about it… but in my head it feels obvious. Like if I’m sitting across from someone, I’ll think, “They can see that side of my face looks lower,” or “My jawline looks soft and uneven,” and then I get really tense.\n\nIt’s not that people are commenting directly. They usually aren’t. That’s part of why I feel a little ridiculous explaining it. But I still feel distressed because I’m convinced they might be noticing it silently. My wife will say, “You look fine,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” and I want to believe her, but it doesn’t really settle it for me. It just keeps coming back, especially if I’m in a situation where I feel visible.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the issue with your jawline is something that others would notice and judge, or do you think it's something that really isn't obvious to anyone but you?\n\nPatient: I go back and forth, but if I’m being honest, in the moment it feels like other people could notice it. Like the sagging or unevenness around my jawline and one side of my face feels so obvious to me once I’ve locked onto it that I have a hard time believing no one else can see it.\n\nAt the same time, I know that sounds… maybe not completely logical, because my wife says she barely sees what I’m talking about, and clients don’t comment on it or seem to react in any obvious way. But that doesn’t really reassure me for long. I’ll think, well, maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they notice but wouldn’t say anything because that would be rude.\n\nIt really got worse after I saw myself in those family photos. I kept seeing this sagging, uneven look, especially around the jaw and one side of my face, and now it’s like I can’t unsee it. So part of me knows it might not be obvious to everyone else, or maybe not as bad as I think, but the stronger feeling is that people probably do notice it and are just not saying anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It feels more like an appearance flaw, I think. Not so much like I’m worried there’s a disease or something physically wrong with my face in a medical sense. It’s more that I get stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw and one side of my face, and then I start thinking that’s what people are seeing when they look at me.

What brings me in is really that I can’t seem to let it go, especially after seeing myself in all those family photos. I kept noticing the same thing over and over — the jawline looking softer, one side looking lower or not matching — and now it sort of jumps out at me. Even though my wife says she barely notices anything, and clients don’t say anything or act like they’re noticing, I still feel like it’s there.

So, yeah, it’s not that I think my face is medically damaged. It’s more that I worry it makes me look… I don’t know, older, unattractive, maybe less put-together than I want to seem. Like people might be taking me less seriously or silently judging me, even if they’re not saying it.
